**C13 · L161:** "A question may fail to pick out its alleged target, assume an incompatible baseline, or combine incompatible requirements."

This sentence describes three ways a question can be in error. The section heading is "Scope, and a question that can be in error". The sentence lists: (i) fail to pick out its alleged target, (ii) assume an incompatible baseline, (iii) combine incompatible requirements.

What does the theory need from it? The theory needs to say a question can itself be defective, in ways connected to its parts (target D, baseline b_0, contract/query). The following sentences handle it: "Its formulation is still an event. Exposing the defect is another question with its own contract."

Definitions used: question p = (D, C, b_0, Q, O_p, ρ_p) at L133–L147; baseline and non-vacuity at L257; when two questions are different: L151.

**C16 · L217:** "For an edit–boundary pair \((a,b)\in C\) actually occurring:"

Content: setup: take a pair in the contract C that actually occurs (in the world/history); the three bullets follow.

Pinned: "(a,b)∈C" (contract pairs, L141), "edit–boundary pair" (L141's term), "actually occurring" (ties to L169 occurrence and L221's H and L223: "one of them must occur"; L582: "at an occurring (a,b)∈C").

Variations:
1. "For an edit–boundary pair \((a,b)\in C\) that actually occurs:" — inflection change; same. Trivial.
2. "For an occurring edit–boundary pair \((a,b)\in C\):" — moves "occurring" before; same content. Trivial? "occurring" without "actually" — drops the *actuality* (the pair must really occur, not merely be admitted). Is "actually" load-bearing? Yes: L223 "one of them must occur", L580–L582 "surprise is defined... at an occurring (a,b)∈C with (a,b)∉H". The word "actually" distinguishes actual occurrence from admission (C admits pairs; they may or may not occur). L159: "admitting a change is not a claim that it can be carried out or come about" — admission ≠ occurrence. So "actually" is load-bearing against the admission/occurrence confusion (S25: physical instantiation is separate). Dropping "actually" breaks the admission/occurrence distinction drawn at L159 and used at L223/L582. Good breakage.
3. "For an edit–boundary pair \((a,b)\in C\) as it actually occurs in the world:" — adds "in the world" — hmm, the theory separates the physical module (Part XII) from explanation (S25). But occurrences are physical (L169: "An occurrence is a physically located carrier"). A pair "occurring" = the edit-boundary pair is instantiated. Adding "in the world" is arguably right but adds content; also might invite the world-matching reading (S28: tentative). Not needed. Break? Not really, but it adds. Hmm.
4. "For an edit–boundary pair \((a,b)\in C\) actually encountered:" — "encountered" is the word for H (L195: "a finite history H⊆C of edit–boundary pairs actually encountered"). Interesting! For a *selected* transport, pairs in H are "actually encountered". But the sentence at L217 is about any transport t ("Let t be a transport to the simulation layer S, with contract C and, where t is selected, history H"). For a declared/constructed transport, there's no H; the pair must "actually occur" regardless. If I wrote "actually encountered", that would tie to selection-history semantics and apply only to selected transports — breaking L223's "Prediction and violation are defined for every transport to the simulation layer" and the setup's neutrality (H only "where t is selected"). Good nameable break: "actually encountered" imports H-membership (L195) and collapses the distinction L223 draws between prediction/violation (all transports) and surprise (selected ones).
5. "For an edit–boundary pair \((a,b)\in C\) actually instantiated:" — "instantiated" is Part XII's term (S26: "Physical possibility comes in only when something is instantiated or transformed"; L159: "whether anyone could carry a change out... is a matter of instantiation and transformation (Part XII)"). It has nothing to do with explanation." So "instantiated" would drag Part XII's vocabulary into the prediction setup. But wait — actually occurring *is* a physical matter (occurrences are physically located carriers, L169). Hmm. But the sentence deliberately says "actually occurring" and the theory's L223 says "one of them must occur". Using "instantiated" would conflate occurrence with instantiation, and L159 carefully separates admitting (explanation-side) from carrying out/coming about (instantiation-side). Is "occurring" = "instantiated"? L375: "a physical interpretation supplying process occurrences, their ports, and the connections actually instantiated" — so histories contain occurrences, and connections are "actually instantiated". Hmm, so instantiation is used for connections in histories. A pair "actually occurring" vs "actually instantiated" — the text's word for pairs is "occurring"/"encountered". Using "instantiated" would tie the pair to Part XII's instantiation, which S25 says has nothing to do with explanation — but prediction/violation is in Part IV which does concern the physical system's transports. Hmm, this is subtle. I think the most defensible and useful reading of "rival wording" is: **a sentence that could replace the candidate and does the same work, differing in some material respect — in the terms used, the structure, or the content stated — where the difference is one a reviser might actually make.** Under this:
- Additions of already-stated content are not rivals (they say more than the sentence's work; they're redundant dressings).
- Synonym swaps and inflections are not rivals (no reviser's doubt is answered).
- Term substitutions (with different text-terms or different technical expressions), content narrowing/broadening, category changes, structural re-derivations of the claim (identity→production, passive→active with attribution) ARE rivals, tested for breakage.

Then:
- C13: term/content changes at two disjuncts that survive → VARIES.
- C14: all term changes (event→occurrence/input/fact/happening) break; content changes (dropping concession, adding clauses) either lose work or say more → HOLDS.
- C15: structural change identity→production ("poses") survives → VARIES. Hmm — is identity→production "a material difference a reviser might make"? Yes (to avoid the loose "exposing X is Y"). OK.
- C16: term changes (occurring→admitted/encountered/instantiated) break → HOLDS.
- C17: term changes (Ans_S→Q_S breaks L177's plural queries; dropping τ,σ breaks (A)/L189; adding fidelity-conditions breaks L223) → HOLDS.
- C18: term change (fidelity→prediction fails / transport breaks) breaks L189/L233–L253/L582; expansion to (F1)/(F2) is a re-spelling and under the sentence's uses (incl. (A)) breaks coverage → HOLDS.
- C19: structural change (passive→active with attribution per L584) survives → VARIES; other changes (kinds/exhaustivity/admit) break.
- C20: content changes (is/Only-omission/creative/meet (G)) break (G)/(N)/L405/L201/L584/L429 → HOLDS.
